Yeah, um… I guess I’m here because I keep getting stuck on this spot on my face. It’s like this rough, kind of sunken-looking patch, especially in photos or when I catch myself in the mirror. One side just looks old and beat-up to me.

People keep telling me they barely notice anything, but I don’t know… I see it every time. I work construction, so I’m out in the sun a lot, and maybe that’s part of it—guys on the job joke about looking weathered, you know, but this feels different to me. I can’t seem to stop looking at it.

People say they don’t really see much, but I keep seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about this patch on your face been affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I guess. And construction jobs don’t exactly wait around for you, you know.\n\nAnd, uh… they take site photos sometimes, progress pictures or crew stuff, and I’ve started dodging those. I’ll kind of step out of the way or pretend I’m busy with something.\n\nEven at home, like family game nights—my wife’s side does those sometimes, cards and snacks and all that—I’ve skipped a few because I just don’t want everyone looking at my face. They’re probably not even doing that, but it feels like they are.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. I’ll kind of step out of the way or pretend I’m busy with something.\n\nEven at home, like family game nights—my wife’s side does those sometimes, cards and snacks and all that—I’ve skipped a few because I just don’t want everyone looking at my face. They’re probably not even doing that, but it feels like they are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you or noticing this patch on your face when they look at you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if they’re judging me exactly, but I feel like they notice it. I can’t stop seeing it myself—that rough, kind of sunken patch—especially in photos or when I catch it in the mirror. It makes that side of my face look old and beat-up to me.\n\nPeople keep saying they barely see anything, and maybe they mean that, but then I’ll be talking to somebody and I’m thinking, are they looking at it? Like on a job site, guys notice everything—crooked cuts, bad concrete, whatever—so I guess my mind goes there with my face too.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're worried about people noticing this patch on your face, is it more that you're concerned about what they might think of you as a person, or is it just that you wish the patch wasn't there?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 52 / construction_laborer / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Preoccupation_with_Appearance\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because I can’t stop seeing this rough, sunken-looking patch on my face in photos and in the mirror, like one side of me looks old and beat-up, even though people keep saying they barely notice anything.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I guess… it’s more that I just wish it wasn’t there. I mean, I worry what people think too, sure, but it starts with me seeing it. Like in a mirror, or in a photo, that rough, sunken-looking patch just jumps out at me, and then I’m thinking one side of my face looks old and beat-up.

And people tell me they barely notice anything, which should help, I know. But it’s like when you’re looking at bad drywall or a crooked frame—you can’t unsee it once you see it. That’s how it feels with my face. It’s just there, and I can’t stop looking at it.

More than I like to admit. Like, I’ll be trying to get out the door for a job and I get stuck checking it in the mirror, changing where I stand in the light, and then I’m late heading to the site. And construction jobs don’t exactly wait around for you, you know.\n\nAnd, uh… they take site photos sometimes, progress pictures or crew stuff, and I’ve started dodging those. I’ll kind of step out of the way or pretend I’m busy with something.\n\nEven at home, like family game nights—my wife’s side does those sometimes, cards and snacks and all that—I’ve skipped a few because I just don’t want everyone looking at my face. They’re probably not even doing that, but it feels like they are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you or noticing this patch on your face when they look at you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if they’re judging me exactly, but I feel like they notice it. I can’t stop seeing it myself—that rough, kind of sunken patch—especially in photos or when I catch it in the mirror. It makes that side of my face look old and beat-up to me.\n\nPeople keep saying they barely see anything, and maybe they mean that, but then I’ll be talking to somebody and I’m thinking, are they looking at it? Like on a job site, guys notice everything—crooked cuts, bad concrete, whatever—so I guess my mind goes there with my face too.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about people noticing this patch on your face, is it more that you're concerned about what they might think of you as a person, or is it just that you wish the patch wasn't there?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s more that I just wish it wasn’t there. I mean, I worry what people think too, sure, but it starts with me seeing it. Like in a mirror, or in a photo, that rough, sunken-looking patch just jumps out at me, and then I’m thinking one side of my face looks old and beat-up.\n\nAnd people tell me they barely notice anything, which should help, I know. But it’s like when you’re looking at bad drywall or a crooked frame—you can’t unsee it once you see it. That’s how it feels with my face. It’s just there, and I can’t stop looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in wanting this patch to be different to just have it look better to yourself, or is it also to avoid any potential social or professional consequences?\n\nPatient: I think mostly I just want it to look better to me, if that makes sense. Like, I’m here because I can’t stop seeing it — that rough, kind of sunken patch on my face. In photos or in the mirror it’s just right there, and it makes one side of me look old and beat-up.\n\nI mean, sure, I don’t want people noticing it at work or anything, but even if nobody said a word… I’d still see it. I can’t seem to stop looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that bother you too?\n\nPatient: Mostly my face. That patch—this rough, kind of sunken-looking area—especially when I see it in photos or the mirror, it’s like one side of me looks old and beat-up. I mean, I work outside, so everybody’s got lines and sun damage and all that, and the guys will joke about it, but this one thing just grabs my eye every time. People say they barely notice it, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Is it just this one patch on your face that bothers you, or are there other specific features about your face that you're concerned with?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly that patch, yeah… the rough, kind of dipped-in looking part. I mean, sometimes I’ll look at the whole side of my face because of it, like it makes that side look older or beat-up compared to the other one. \n\nPhotos are the worst—well, mirrors too, depending on the lighting. On job sites we’re always out in the sun, so everybody’s got weathered skin, and the guys laugh about it, but I don’t know… this doesn’t feel like normal lines to me. People say they don’t really see much, but I keep seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about this patch on your face been affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I guess. More than I like to admit. Like, I’ll be trying to get out the door for a job and I get stuck checking it in the mirror, changing where I stand in the light, and then I’m late heading to the site. And construction jobs don’t exactly wait around for you, you know.\n\nAnd, uh… they take site photos sometimes, progress pictures or crew stuff, and I’ve started dodging those. I’ll kind of step out of the way or pretend I’m busy with something.\n\nEven at home, like family game nights—my wife’s side does those sometimes, cards and snacks and all that—I’ve skipped a few because I just don’t want everyone looking at my face. They’re probably not even doing that, but it feels like they are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you or noticing this patch on your face when they look at you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if they’re judging me exactly, but I feel like they notice it. I can’t stop seeing it myself—that rough, kind of sunken patch—especially in photos or when I catch it in the mirror. It makes that side of my face look old and beat-up to me.\n\nPeople keep saying they barely see anything, and maybe they mean that, but then I’ll be talking to somebody and I’m thinking, are they looking at it? Like on a job site, guys notice everything—crooked cuts, bad concrete, whatever—so I guess my mind goes there with my face too.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about people noticing this patch on your face, is it more that you're concerned about what they might think of you as a person, or is it just that you wish the patch wasn't there?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s more that I just wish it wasn’t there. I mean, I worry what people think too, sure, but it starts with me seeing it. Like in a mirror, or in a photo, that rough, sunken-looking patch just jumps out at me, and then I’m thinking one side of my face looks old and beat-up.\n\nAnd people tell me they barely notice anything, which should help, I know. But it’s like when you’re looking at bad drywall or a crooked frame—you can’t unsee it once you see it. That’s how it feels with my face. It’s just there, and I can’t stop looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in wanting this patch to be different to just have it look better to yourself, or is it also to avoid any potential social or professional consequences?\n\nPatient: I think mostly I just want it to look better to me, if that makes sense. Like, I’m here because I can’t stop seeing it — that rough, kind of sunken patch on my face. In photos or in the mirror it’s just right there, and it makes one side of me look old and beat-up.\n\nI mean, sure, I don’t want people noticing it at work or anything, but even if nobody said a word… I’d still see it. My wife says it’s barely anything, and people tell me they don’t really notice, but that almost makes me feel crazier because I’m standing there staring at it like it’s the first thing on my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if this patch on your face were to disappear?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, I’d like to think so. If that rough, sunken-looking patch was gone, I feel like I wouldn’t keep getting stuck on my face every time I look in the mirror or see a photo.\n\nBut then, you know, I do construction, and sometimes you fix one part of a wall and then another spot starts looking off, so… I don’t know. But this patch is the thing I can’t stop seeing right now. It makes one side of my face look old and beat-up to me, even though everyone keeps saying they barely notice anything. So yeah, I think I’d be a lot more satisfied if it just wasn’t there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Not really other parts, no. It’s mostly my face—this one spot.

Like before work, or if we’re supposed to go to my sister’s game night, I’ll end up in the bathroom mirror too long, turning my face this way and that under the light. Bathrooms are the worst for it, you know, that overhead lighting makes everything look rough. I’ll rub at the spot too, like I’m trying to smooth it out, which I know doesn’t really do anything.

Sometimes I’ll wear my hat lower if I feel like it looks bad. Or I’ll start thinking of some excuse not to go, like I’m tired from work or my back’s acting up, when really it’s because I think that patch is too obvious. But other body parts, not so much. It’s really that face thing.

But it’s like when you’re looking at bad drywall or a crooked frame—you can’t unsee it once you see it. That’s how it feels with my face. It’s just there, and I can’t stop looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in wanting this patch to be different to just have it look better to yourself, or is it also to avoid any potential social or professional consequences?\n\nPatient: I think mostly I just want it to look better to me, if that makes sense. Like, I’m here because I can’t stop seeing it — that rough, kind of sunken patch on my face. In photos or in the mirror it’s just right there, and it makes one side of me look old and beat-up.\n\nI mean, sure, I don’t want people noticing it at work or anything, but even if nobody said a word… I’d still see it. My wife says it’s barely anything, and people tell me they don’t really notice, but that almost makes me feel crazier because I’m standing there staring at it like it’s the first thing on my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if this patch on your face were to disappear?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, I’d like to think so. If that rough, sunken-looking patch was gone, I feel like I wouldn’t keep getting stuck on my face every time I look in the mirror or see a photo.\n\nBut then, you know, I do construction, and sometimes you fix one part of a wall and then another spot starts looking off, so… I don’t know. But this patch is the thing I can’t stop seeing right now. It makes one side of my face look old and beat-up to me, even though everyone keeps saying they barely notice anything. So yeah, I think I’d be a lot more satisfied if it just wasn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeatedly checking or touching other parts of your body besides your face?\n\nPatient: Not really other parts, no. It’s mostly my face—this one spot.\n\nLike before work, or if we’re supposed to go to my sister’s game night, I’ll end up in the bathroom mirror too long, turning my face this way and that under the light. Bathrooms are the worst for it, you know, that overhead lighting makes everything look rough. I’ll rub at the spot too, like I’m trying to smooth it out, which I know doesn’t really do anything.\n\nSometimes I’ll wear my hat lower if I feel like it looks bad. Or I’ll start thinking of some excuse not to go, like I’m tired from work or my back’s acting up, when really it’s because I think that patch is too obvious. But other body parts, not so much. It’s really that face thing.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I mean, I guess it has. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds kind of ridiculous when I say it out loud.

I’ve been late getting out the door for jobs because I’m in the mirror messing with it, checking it under different light, and then suddenly I’m rushing to the construction site. So yeah, it’s been affecting things.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I’m here because I keep getting stuck on this spot on my face. It’s like this rough, kind of sunken-looking patch, especially in photos or when I catch myself in the mirror. One side just looks old and beat-up to me.\n\nPeople keep telling me they barely notice anything, but I don’t know… I see it every time. I work construction, so I’m out in the sun a lot, and maybe that’s part of it—guys on the job joke about looking weathered, you know, but this feels different to me. I can’t seem to stop looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that bother you too?\n\nPatient: Mostly my face. That patch—this rough, kind of sunken-looking area—especially when I see it in photos or the mirror, it’s like one side of me looks old and beat-up. I mean, I work outside, so everybody’s got lines and sun damage and all that, and the guys will joke about it, but this one thing just grabs my eye every time. People say they barely notice it, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Is it just this one patch on your face that bothers you, or are there other specific features about your face that you're concerned with?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly that patch, yeah… the rough, kind of dipped-in looking part. I mean, sometimes I’ll look at the whole side of my face because of it, like it makes that side look older or beat-up compared to the other one. \n\nPhotos are the worst—well, mirrors too, depending on the lighting. On job sites we’re always out in the sun, so everybody’s got weathered skin, and the guys laugh about it, but I don’t know… this doesn’t feel like normal lines to me. People say they don’t really see much, but I keep seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about this patch on your face been affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I guess. More than I like to admit. Like, I’ll be trying to get out the door for a job and I get stuck checking it in the mirror, changing where I stand in the light, and then I’m late heading to the site. And construction jobs don’t exactly wait around for you, you know.\n\nAnd, uh… they take site photos sometimes, progress pictures or crew stuff, and I’ve started dodging those. I’ll kind of step out of the way or pretend I’m busy with something.\n\nEven at home, like family game nights—my wife’s side does those sometimes, cards and snacks and all that—I’ve skipped a few because I just don’t want everyone looking at my face. They’re probably not even doing that, but it feels like they are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you or noticing this patch on your face when they look at you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if they’re judging me exactly, but I feel like they notice it. If that rough, sunken-looking patch was gone, I feel like I wouldn’t keep getting stuck on my face every time I look in the mirror or see a photo.\n\nBut then, you know, I do construction, and sometimes you fix one part of a wall and then another spot starts looking off, so… I don’t know. But this patch is the thing I can’t stop seeing right now. It makes one side of my face look old and beat-up to me, even though everyone keeps saying they barely notice anything. So yeah, I think I’d be a lot more satisfied if it just wasn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeatedly checking or touching other parts of your body besides your face?\n\nPatient: Not really other parts, no. It’s mostly my face—this one spot.\n\nLike before work, or if we’re supposed to go to my sister’s game night, I’ll end up in the bathroom mirror too long, turning my face this way and that under the light. Bathrooms are the worst for it, you know, that overhead lighting makes everything look rough. I’ll rub at the spot too, like I’m trying to smooth it out, which I know doesn’t really do anything.\n\nSometimes I’ll wear my hat lower if I feel like it looks bad. Or I’ll start thinking of some excuse not to go, like I’m tired from work or my back’s acting up, when really it’s because I think that patch is too obvious. But other body parts, not so much. It’s really that face thing.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face affected your ability to work or interact with others in a way that you feel is impacting your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess it has. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds kind of ridiculous when I say it out loud.\n\nI’ve been late getting out the door for jobs because I’m in the mirror messing with it, checking it under different light, and then suddenly I’m rushing to the construction site. And at work, if they’re taking site photos, I kind of hang back or turn away. I don’t want my face showing up looking like that.\n\nAnd with family stuff too… my sister does these game nights, and sometimes I’ll just skip them or make up some reason, like I’m wiped out from work. Which, I mean, I am tired a lot—construction will do that to you, especially at my age—but really it’s more that I don’t want to sit there feeling like everybody can see that patch on my face. So yeah, it’s been affecting things.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. It’s more specific, I think. It’s that patch. I mean, I don’t love getting older—who does, right? Working construction, being out in the sun, you start noticing every line and dent eventually. My hands look like sandpaper half the time, but that doesn’t bother me the same way.

With my face, it’s this rough, kind of sunken-looking spot. In the mirror or in photos, my eye goes right to it, like one side of my face looks old and beat-up. Other people keep telling me they barely notice it, but I don’t know… to me it’s all I can see.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I’m here because I keep getting stuck on this spot on my face. It’s like this rough, kind of sunken-looking patch, especially in photos or when I catch myself in the mirror. One side just looks old and beat-up to me.\n\nPeople keep telling me they barely notice anything, but I don’t know… I see it every time. I work construction, so I’m out in the sun a lot, and maybe that’s part of it—guys on the job joke about looking weathered, you know, but this feels different to me. I can’t seem to stop looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that bother you too?\n\nPatient: Mostly my face. That patch—this rough, kind of sunken-looking area—especially when I see it in photos or the mirror, it’s like one side of me looks old and beat-up. I mean, I work outside, so everybody’s got lines and sun damage and all that, and the guys will joke about it, but this one thing just grabs my eye every time. People say they barely notice it, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Is it just this one patch on your face that bothers you, or are there other specific features about your face that you're concerned with?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly that patch, yeah… the rough, kind of dipped-in looking part. I mean, sometimes I’ll look at the whole side of my face because of it, like it makes that side look older or beat-up compared to the other one. \n\nPhotos are the worst—well, mirrors too, depending on the lighting. On job sites we’re always out in the sun, so everybody’s got weathered skin, and the guys laugh about it, but I don’t know… this doesn’t feel like normal lines to me. People say they don’t really see much, but I keep seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about this patch on your face been affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I guess. More than I like to admit. Like, I’ll be trying to get out the door for a job and I get stuck checking it in the mirror, changing where I stand in the light, and then I’m late heading to the site. And construction jobs don’t exactly wait around for you, you know.\n\nAnd, uh… they take site photos sometimes, progress pictures or crew stuff, and I’ve started dodging those. I’ll kind of step out of the way or pretend I’m busy with something.\n\nEven at home, like family game nights—my wife’s side does those sometimes, cards and snacks and all that—I’ve skipped a few because I just don’t want everyone looking at my face. They’re probably not even doing that, but it feels like they are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you or noticing this patch on your face when they look at you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if they’re judging me exactly, but I feel like they notice it. I can’t stop seeing it myself—that rough, kind of sunken patch—especially in photos or when I catch it in the mirror. It makes that side of my face look old and beat-up to me.\n\nPeople keep saying they barely see anything, and maybe they mean that, but then I’ll be talking to somebody and I’m thinking, are they looking at it? Like on a job site, guys notice everything—crooked cuts, bad concrete, whatever—so I guess my mind goes there with my face too.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about people noticing this patch on your face, is it more that you're concerned about what they might think of you as a person, or is it just that you wish the patch wasn't there?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s more that I just wish it wasn’t there. I mean, I worry what people think too, sure, but it starts with me seeing it. Like in a mirror, or in a photo, that rough, sunken-looking patch just jumps out at me, and then I’m thinking one side of my face looks old and beat-up.\n\nAnd people tell me they barely notice anything, which should help, I know. But it’s like when you’re looking at bad drywall or a crooked frame—you can’t unsee it once you see it. That’s how it feels with my face. It’s just there, and I can’t stop looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in wanting this patch to be different to just have it look better to yourself, or is it also to avoid any potential social or professional consequences?\n\nPatient: I think mostly I just want it to look better to me, if that makes sense. Like, I’m here because I can’t stop seeing it — that rough, kind of sunken patch on my face. In photos or in the mirror it’s just right there, and it makes one side of me look old and beat-up.\n\nI mean, sure, I don’t want people noticing it at work or anything, but even if nobody said a word… I’d still see it. My wife says it’s barely anything, and people tell me they don’t really notice, but that almost makes me feel crazier because I’m standing there staring at it like it’s the first thing on my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if this patch on your face were to disappear?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, I’d like to think so. If that rough, sunken-looking patch was gone, I feel like I wouldn’t keep getting stuck on my face every time I look in the mirror or see a photo.\n\nBut then, you know, I do construction, and sometimes you fix one part of a wall and then another spot starts looking off, so… I don’t know. It’s just there, and I can’t stop looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in wanting this patch to be different to just have it look better to yourself, or is it also to avoid any potential social or professional consequences?\n\nPatient: I think mostly I just want it to look better to me, if that makes sense. Like, I’m here because I can’t stop seeing it — that rough, kind of sunken patch on my face. In photos or in the mirror it’s just right there, and it makes one side of me look old and beat-up.\n\nI mean, sure, I don’t want people noticing it at work or anything, but even if nobody said a word… I’d still see it. My wife says it’s barely anything, and people tell me they don’t really notice, but that almost makes me feel crazier because I’m standing there staring at it like it’s the first thing on my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if this patch on your face were to disappear?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, I’d like to think so. If that rough, sunken-looking patch was gone, I feel like I wouldn’t keep getting stuck on my face every time I look in the mirror or see a photo.\n\nBut then, you know, I do construction, and sometimes you fix one part of a wall and then another spot starts looking off, so… I don’t know. But this patch is the thing I can’t stop seeing right now. It makes one side of my face look old and beat-up to me, even though everyone keeps saying they barely notice anything. So yeah, I think I’d be a lot more satisfied if it just wasn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeatedly checking or touching other parts of your body besides your face?\n\nPatient: Not really other parts, no. It’s mostly my face—this one spot.\n\nLike before work, or if we’re supposed to go to my sister’s game night, I’ll end up in the bathroom mirror too long, turning my face this way and that under the light. Bathrooms are the worst for it, you know, that overhead lighting makes everything look rough. I’ll rub at the spot too, like I’m trying to smooth it out, which I know doesn’t really do anything.\n\nSometimes I’ll wear my hat lower if I feel like it looks bad. Or I’ll start thinking of some excuse not to go, like I’m tired from work or my back’s acting up, when really it’s because I think that patch is too obvious. But other body parts, not so much. It’s really that face thing.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face affected your ability to work or interact with others in a way that you feel is impacting your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess it has. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds kind of ridiculous when I say it out loud.\n\nI’ve been late getting out the door for jobs because I’m in the mirror messing with it, checking it under different light, and then suddenly I’m rushing to the construction site. And at work, if they’re taking site photos, I kind of hang back or turn away. I don’t want my face showing up looking like that.\n\nAnd with family stuff too… my sister does these game nights, and sometimes I’ll just skip them or make up some reason, like I’m wiped out from work. Which, I mean, I am tired a lot—construction will do that to you, especially at my age—but really it’s more that I don’t want to sit there feeling like everybody can see that patch on my face. So yeah, it’s been affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the patch on your face that bothers you, or is it more that you just don't like how your face looks in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more specific, I think. It’s that patch. I mean, I don’t love getting older—who does, right? Working construction, being out in the sun, you start noticing every line and dent eventually. My hands look like sandpaper half the time, but that doesn’t bother me the same way.\n\nWith my face, it’s this rough, kind of sunken-looking spot. In the mirror or in photos, my eye goes right to it, like one side of my face looks old and beat-up. Other people keep telling me they barely notice it, but I don’t know… to me it’s all I can see.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I’m here because I keep getting stuck on this spot on my face. More than I like to admit. Like, I’ll be trying to get out the door for a job and I get stuck checking it in the mirror, changing where I stand in the light, and then I’m late heading to the site. And construction jobs don’t exactly wait around for you, you know.\n\nAnd, uh… they take site photos sometimes, progress pictures or crew stuff, and I’ve started dodging those. I’ll kind of step out of the way or pretend I’m busy with something.\n\nEven at home, like family game nights—my wife’s side does those sometimes, cards and snacks and all that—I’ve skipped a few because I just don’t want everyone looking at my face. They’re probably not even doing that, but it feels like they are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you or noticing this patch on your face when they look at you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if they’re judging me exactly, but I feel like they notice it. I can’t stop seeing it myself—that rough, kind of sunken patch—especially in photos or when I catch it in the mirror. It makes that side of my face look old and beat-up to me.\n\nPeople keep saying they barely see anything, and maybe they mean that, but then I’ll be talking to somebody and I’m thinking, are they looking at it? Like on a job site, guys notice everything—crooked cuts, bad concrete, whatever—so I guess my mind goes there with my face too.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about people noticing this patch on your face, is it more that you're concerned about what they might think of you as a person, or is it just that you wish the patch wasn't there?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s more that I just wish it wasn’t there. I mean, I worry what people think too, sure, but it starts with me seeing it. Like in a mirror, or in a photo, that rough, sunken-looking patch just jumps out at me, and then I’m thinking one side of my face looks old and beat-up.\n\nAnd people tell me they barely notice anything, which should help, I know. But it’s like when you’re looking at bad drywall or a crooked frame—you can’t unsee it once you see it. That’s how it feels with my face. It’s just there, and I can’t stop looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in wanting this patch to be different to just have it look better to yourself, or is it also to avoid any potential social or professional consequences?\n\nPatient: I think mostly I just want it to look better to me, if that makes sense. Like, I’m here because I can’t stop seeing it — that rough, kind of sunken patch on my face. In photos or in the mirror it’s just right there, and it makes one side of me look old and beat-up.\n\nI mean, sure, I don’t want people noticing it at work or anything, but even if nobody said a word… I’d still see it. My wife says it’s barely anything, and people tell me they don’t really notice, but that almost makes me feel crazier because I’m standing there staring at it like it’s the first thing on my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if this patch on your face were to disappear?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, I’d like to think so. If that rough, sunken-looking patch was gone, I feel like I wouldn’t keep getting stuck on my face every time I look in the mirror or see a photo.\n\nBut then, you know, I do construction, and sometimes you fix one part of a wall and then another spot starts looking off, so… I don’t know. But this patch is the thing I can’t stop seeing right now. It makes one side of my face look old and beat-up to me, even though everyone keeps saying they barely notice anything. So yeah, I think I’d be a lot more satisfied if it just wasn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeatedly checking or touching other parts of your body besides your face?\n\nPatient: Not really other parts, no. It’s mostly my face—this one spot.\n\nLike before work, or if we’re supposed to go to my sister’s game night, I’ll end up in the bathroom mirror too long, turning my face this way and that under the light. Bathrooms are the worst for it, you know, that overhead lighting makes everything look rough. I’ll rub at the spot too, like I’m trying to smooth it out, which I know doesn’t really do anything.\n\nSometimes I’ll wear my hat lower if I feel like it looks bad. Or I’ll start thinking of some excuse not to go, like I’m tired from work or my back’s acting up, when really it’s because I think that patch is too obvious. But other body parts, not so much. It’s really that face thing.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face affected your ability to work or interact with others in a way that you feel is impacting your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess it has. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds kind of ridiculous when I say it out loud.\n\nI’ve been late getting out the door for jobs because I’m in the mirror messing with it, checking it under different light, and then suddenly I’m rushing to the construction site. And at work, if they’re taking site photos, I kind of hang back or turn away. I don’t want my face showing up looking like that.\n\nAnd with family stuff too… my sister does these game nights, and sometimes I’ll just skip them or make up some reason, like I’m wiped out from work. Which, I mean, I am tired a lot—construction will do that to you, especially at my age—but really it’s more that I don’t want to sit there feeling like everybody can see that patch on my face. So yeah, it’s been affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the patch on your face that bothers you, or is it more that you just don't like how your face looks in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more specific, I think. It’s that patch. I mean, I don’t love getting older—who does, right? Working construction, being out in the sun, you start noticing every line and dent eventually. My hands look like sandpaper half the time, but that doesn’t bother me the same way.\n\nWith my face, it’s this rough, kind of sunken-looking spot. In the mirror or in photos, my eye goes right to it, like one side of my face looks old and beat-up. Other people keep telling me they barely notice it, but I don’t know… to me it’s all I can see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that patch on your face looks the way you think it does to other people?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, people say they barely notice it, and I hear that, but then I catch myself in a photo or in the bathroom mirror and it’s just right there to me. That rough, kind of sunken patch.\n\nIt’s like one side of my face looks older, beat-up, like I’ve been out in the sun and wind too many years. Which, I mean, I have—construction will do that, you’re outside all day, hard hat rubbing, dust everywhere. But this one spot just looks bad to me. Other people keep saying it’s nothing, or they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I can’t stop seeing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

Like, I’m here because I can’t stop seeing it — that rough, kind of sunken patch on my face. In photos or in the mirror it’s just right there, and it makes one side of me look old and beat-up.\n\nI mean, sure, I don’t want people noticing it at work or anything, but even if nobody said a word… I’d still see it. If that rough, sunken-looking patch was gone, I feel like I wouldn’t keep getting stuck on my face every time I look in the mirror or see a photo.\n\nBut then, you know, I do construction, and sometimes you fix one part of a wall and then another spot starts looking off, so… I don’t know. It’s mostly my face—this one spot.\n\nLike before work, or if we’re supposed to go to my sister’s game night, I’ll end up in the bathroom mirror too long, turning my face this way and that under the light. Bathrooms are the worst for it, you know, that overhead lighting makes everything look rough. I’ll rub at the spot too, like I’m trying to smooth it out, which I know doesn’t really do anything.\n\nSometimes I’ll wear my hat lower if I feel like it looks bad. Or I’ll start thinking of some excuse not to go, like I’m tired from work or my back’s acting up, when really it’s because I think that patch is too obvious. But other body parts, not so much. It’s really that face thing.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face affected your ability to work or interact with others in a way that you feel is impacting your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess it has. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds kind of ridiculous when I say it out loud.\n\nI’ve been late getting out the door for jobs because I’m in the mirror messing with it, checking it under different light, and then suddenly I’m rushing to the construction site. And at work, if they’re taking site photos, I kind of hang back or turn away. I don’t want my face showing up looking like that.\n\nAnd with family stuff too… my sister does these game nights, and sometimes I’ll just skip them or make up some reason, like I’m wiped out from work. Which, I mean, I am tired a lot—construction will do that to you, especially at my age—but really it’s more that I don’t want to sit there feeling like everybody can see that patch on my face. So yeah, it’s been affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the patch on your face that bothers you, or is it more that you just don't like how your face looks in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more specific, I think. It’s that patch. I mean, I don’t love getting older—who does, right? Working construction, being out in the sun, you start noticing every line and dent eventually. My hands look like sandpaper half the time, but that doesn’t bother me the same way.\n\nWith my face, it’s this rough, kind of sunken-looking spot. In the mirror or in photos, my eye goes right to it, like one side of my face looks old and beat-up. Other people keep telling me they barely notice it, but I don’t know… to me it’s all I can see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that patch on your face looks the way you think it does to other people?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, people say they barely notice it, and I hear that, but then I catch myself in a photo or in the bathroom mirror and it’s just right there to me. That rough, kind of sunken patch.\n\nIt’s like one side of my face looks older, beat-up, like I’ve been out in the sun and wind too many years. Which, I mean, I have—construction will do that, you’re outside all day, hard hat rubbing, dust everywhere. But this one spot just looks bad to me. Other people keep saying it’s nothing, or they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that patch on your face is actually there, or is it something you just see when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I think it’s there… I mean, I don’t think I’m making it up out of nothing. When I look in the mirror, especially in certain light, or if I see a photo of myself, I can see that rough, kind of sunken-looking patch. It’s like one side of my face just looks old and beat-up.\n\nBut then other people say they barely notice anything, so I don’t know if it’s as bad as I’m seeing it. I work construction, so I’m out in the sun a lot, and maybe that’s part of it—guys on the job joke about looking weathered, you know, but this feels different to me. I can’t seem to stop looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that bother you too?\n\nPatient: Mostly my face. That patch—this rough, kind of sunken-looking area—especially when I see it in photos or the mirror, it’s like one side of me looks old and beat-up. I mean, I work outside, so everybody’s got lines and sun damage and all that, and the guys will joke about it, but this one thing just grabs my eye every time. People say they barely notice it, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Is it just this one patch on your face that bothers you, or are there other specific features about your face that you're concerned with?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly that patch, yeah… the rough, kind of dipped-in looking part. I mean, sometimes I’ll look at the whole side of my face because of it, like it makes that side look older or beat-up compared to the other one. \n\nPhotos are the worst—well, mirrors too, depending on the lighting. On job sites we’re always out in the sun, so everybody’s got weathered skin, and the guys laugh about it, but I don’t know… this doesn’t feel like normal lines to me. People say they don’t really see much, but I keep seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about this patch on your face been affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I guess. More than I like to admit. Like, I’ll be trying to get out the door for a job and I get stuck checking it in the mirror, changing where I stand in the light, and then I’m late heading to the site. And construction jobs don’t exactly wait around for you, you know.\n\nAnd, uh… they take site photos sometimes, progress pictures or crew stuff, and I’ve started dodging those. I’ll kind of step out of the way or pretend I’m busy with something.\n\nEven at home, like family game nights—my wife’s side does those sometimes, cards and snacks and all that—I’ve skipped a few because I just don’t want everyone looking at my face. They’re probably not even doing that, but it feels like they are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you or noticing this patch on your face when they look at you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if they’re judging me exactly, but I feel like they notice it. I can’t stop seeing it myself—that rough, kind of sunken patch—especially in photos or when I catch it in the mirror. It makes that side of my face look old and beat-up to me.\n\nPeople keep saying they barely see anything, and maybe they mean that, but then I’ll be talking to somebody and I’m thinking, are they looking at it? Like on a job site, guys notice everything—crooked cuts, bad concrete, whatever—so I guess my mind goes there with my face too.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about people noticing this patch on your face, is it more that you're concerned about what they might think of you as a person, or is it just that you wish the patch wasn't there?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s more that I just wish it wasn’t there. I mean, I worry what people think too, sure, but it starts with me seeing it. Like in a mirror, or in a photo, that rough, sunken-looking patch just jumps out at me, and then I’m thinking one side of my face looks old and beat-up.\n\nAnd people tell me they barely notice anything, which should help, I know. But it’s like when you’re looking at bad drywall or a crooked frame—you can’t unsee it once you see it. That’s how it feels with my face. It’s just there, and I can’t stop looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in wanting this patch to be different to just have it look better to yourself, or is it also to avoid any potential social or professional consequences?\n\nPatient: I think mostly I just want it to look better to me, if that makes sense. Like, I’m here because I can’t stop seeing it — that rough, kind of sunken patch on my face. In photos or in the mirror it’s just right there, and it makes one side of me look old and beat-up.\n\nI mean, sure, I don’t want people noticing it at work or anything, but even if nobody said a word… I’d still see it. My wife says it’s barely anything, and people tell me they don’t really notice, but that almost makes me feel crazier because I’m standing there staring at it like it’s the first thing on my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if this patch on your face were to disappear?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, I’d like to think so. If that rough, sunken-looking patch was gone, I feel like I wouldn’t keep getting stuck on my face every time I look in the mirror or see a photo.\n\nBut then, you know, I do construction, and sometimes you fix one part of a wall and then another spot starts looking off, so… I don’t know. But this patch is the thing I can’t stop seeing right now. It makes one side of my face look old and beat-up to me, even though everyone keeps saying they barely notice anything. So yeah, I think I’d be a lot more satisfied if it just wasn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeatedly checking or touching other parts of your body besides your face?\n\nPatient: Not really other parts, no. It’s mostly my face—this one spot.\n\nLike before work, or if we’re supposed to go to my sister’s game night, I’ll end up in the bathroom mirror too long, turning my face this way and that under the light. Bathrooms are the worst for it, you know, that overhead lighting makes everything look rough. I’ll rub at the spot too, like I’m trying to smooth it out, which I know doesn’t really do anything.\n\nSometimes I’ll wear my hat lower if I feel like it looks bad. Or I’ll start thinking of some excuse not to go, like I’m tired from work or my back’s acting up, when really it’s because I think that patch is too obvious. But other body parts, not so much. It’s really that face thing.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face affected your ability to work or interact with others in a way that you feel is impacting your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess it has. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds kind of ridiculous when I say it out loud.\n\nI’ve been late getting out the door for jobs because I’m in the mirror messing with it, checking it under different light, and then suddenly I’m rushing to the construction site. And at work, if they’re taking site photos, I kind of hang back or turn away. I don’t want my face showing up looking like that.\n\nAnd with family stuff too… my sister does these game nights, and sometimes I’ll just skip them or make up some reason, like I’m wiped out from work. Which, I mean, I am tired a lot—construction will do that to you, especially at my age—but really it’s more that I don’t want to sit there feeling like everybody can see that patch on my face. So yeah, it’s been affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the patch on your face that bothers you, or is it more that you just don't like how your face looks in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more specific, I think. It’s that patch. I mean, I don’t love getting older—who does, right? Working construction, being out in the sun, you start noticing every line and dent eventually. My hands look like sandpaper half the time, but that doesn’t bother me the same way.\n\nWith my face, it’s this rough, kind of sunken-looking spot. In the mirror or in photos, my eye goes right to it, like one side of my face looks old and beat-up. Other people keep telling me they barely notice it, but I don’t know… to me it’s all I can see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that patch on your face looks the way you think it does to other people?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, people say they barely notice it, and I hear that, but then I catch myself in a photo or in the bathroom mirror and it’s just right there to me. That rough, kind of sunken patch.\n\nIt’s like one side of my face looks older, beat-up, like I’ve been out in the sun and wind too many years. Which, I mean, I have—construction will do that, you’re outside all day, hard hat rubbing, dust everywhere. But this one spot just looks bad to me. Other people keep saying it’s nothing, or they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that patch on your face is actually there, or is it something you just see when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I think it’s there… I mean, I don’t think I’m making it up out of nothing. When I look in the mirror, especially in certain light, or if I see a photo of myself, I can see that rough, kind of sunken-looking patch. It’s like one side of my face just looks old and beat-up.\n\nBut then other people say they barely notice anything, so I don’t know if it’s as bad as I’m seeing it. You know, I’ve worked outside for years, sun and dust and all that, so part of me thinks, well, of course my face is going to show it. But this one spot… I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that patch on your face would still be there if you couldn't see yourself in mirrors or photos?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, I think it would still be there. I mean, I’m here because I can’t stop seeing it when I do see myself — in the mirror, in photos, that rough, kind of sunken patch. It’s like one side of my face looks old and beat-up.\n\nIf I couldn’t look, maybe I wouldn’t be staring at it all the time, you know? Like when I’m on a job and I’m busy hauling stuff or cutting boards, I’m not thinking about my face every second. But then I catch my reflection in a truck window or someone shows me a picture, and there it is again. Other people say they barely notice anything, but to me it’s just… right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face would look different to you if nobody else could see it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe if nobody else could see it, I’d still see it. That’s kind of the problem, I guess. I’m here because I can’t stop noticing that rough, sunken-looking patch when I look in the mirror or see myself in photos.\n\nPeople keep telling me it’s barely anything, and maybe they’re right, but to me it makes that side of my face look old, beat-up. Like after years on job sites, sun hitting you all day, dust sticking to your skin—sorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked. I just mean… even if no one else saw it, I think I’d still be stuck seeing it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

Your goal is to answer the doctor's question naturally, using the\ninformation you are given.\n\nAnswer with the [Targeted Info], told in the way [Conversation Style] describes.\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Do you think that patch on your face is actually there, or is it something you just see when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I think it’s there… I mean, I don’t think I’m making it up out of nothing. When I look in the mirror, especially in certain light, or if I see a photo of myself, I can see that rough, kind of sunken-looking patch. It’s like one side of my face just looks old and beat-up.\n\nBut then other people say they barely notice anything, so I don’t know if it’s as bad as I’m seeing it. You know, I’ve worked outside for years, sun and dust and all that, so part of me thinks, well, of course my face is going to show it. But this one spot… I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that patch on your face would still be there if you couldn't see yourself in mirrors or photos?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, I think it would still be there. I mean, I’m here because I can’t stop seeing it when I do see myself — in the mirror, in photos, that rough, kind of sunken patch. It’s like one side of my face looks old and beat-up.\n\nIf I couldn’t look, maybe I wouldn’t be staring at it all the time, you know? Like when I’m on a job and I’m busy hauling stuff or cutting boards, I’m not thinking about my face every second. But then I catch my reflection in a truck window or someone shows me a picture, and there it is again. Other people say they barely notice anything, but to me it’s just… right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face would look different to you if nobody else could see it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe if nobody else could see it, I’d still see it. That’s kind of the problem, I guess. I’m here because I can’t stop noticing that rough, sunken-looking patch when I look in the mirror or see myself in photos.\n\nPeople keep telling me it’s barely anything, and maybe they’re right, but to me it makes that side of my face look old, beat-up. Like after years on job sites, sun hitting you all day, dust sticking to your skin—sorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked. I just mean… even if no one else saw it, I think I’d still be stuck seeing it.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you think a dermatologist or plastic surgeon could fix the patch on your face to your satisfaction?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 52 / construction_laborer / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Tangent patient.\nA patient who goes off on tangents may\n1) begin answering the doctor's question but quickly shift to an unrelated topic,\n2) frequently add irrelevant personal anecdotes or experiences while answering the question, and\n3) have difficulty remaining focused on the doctor's question, potentially requiring the doctor to repeat the question or redirect the conversation to obtain clear clinical information.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t know. I mean, I’ve thought about it, sure, like maybe there’s some cream or laser thing or… I don’t know what they even do for that. My cousin had something done on his nose once, not the same thing, he got hit playing softball years ago and it always bothered him—

But for me, I’m not sure if I’d be satisfied. Maybe if someone could smooth it out or make it look less sunken, that would help. But then I worry I’d still look at it and think it’s not right. I guess I’d have to talk to someone who actually knows skin, because I’m just looking at it every day and driving myself nuts with it.

---------- turn 16 | doctor:inference | doctor.build_inference_prompt() | model=meta-llama/Llama-3.3-70B-Instruct ----------

    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF45.2, F42.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Yeah, um… I guess I’m here because I keep getting stuck on this spot on my face. It’s like this rough, kind of sunken-looking patch, especially in photos or when I catch myself in the mirror. One side just looks old and beat-up to me.\n\nPeople keep telling me they barely notice anything, but I don’t know… I see it every time. I work construction, so I’m out in the sun a lot, and maybe that’s part of it—guys on the job joke about looking weathered, you know, but this feels different to me. I can’t seem to stop looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Is it just your face that you're concerned about, or are there other parts of your body that bother you too?\n\nPatient: Mostly my face. That patch—this rough, kind of sunken-looking area—especially when I see it in photos or the mirror, it’s like one side of me looks old and beat-up. I mean, I work outside, so everybody’s got lines and sun damage and all that, and the guys will joke about it, but this one thing just grabs my eye every time. People say they barely notice it, but I do.\n\nDoctor: Is it just this one patch on your face that bothers you, or are there other specific features about your face that you're concerned with?\n\nPatient: It’s mainly that patch, yeah… the rough, kind of dipped-in looking part. I mean, sometimes I’ll look at the whole side of my face because of it, like it makes that side look older or beat-up compared to the other one. \n\nPhotos are the worst—well, mirrors too, depending on the lighting. On job sites we’re always out in the sun, so everybody’s got weathered skin, and the guys laugh about it, but I don’t know… this doesn’t feel like normal lines to me. People say they don’t really see much, but I keep seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about this patch on your face been affecting your daily life or relationships in any way?\n\nPatient: Yeah… it has, I guess. More than I like to admit. Like, I’ll be trying to get out the door for a job and I get stuck checking it in the mirror, changing where I stand in the light, and then I’m late heading to the site. And construction jobs don’t exactly wait around for you, you know.\n\nAnd, uh… they take site photos sometimes, progress pictures or crew stuff, and I’ve started dodging those. I’ll kind of step out of the way or pretend I’m busy with something.\n\nEven at home, like family game nights—my wife’s side does those sometimes, cards and snacks and all that—I’ve skipped a few because I just don’t want everyone looking at my face. They’re probably not even doing that, but it feels like they are.\n\nDoctor: Do you think people are judging you or noticing this patch on your face when they look at you?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I don’t know if they’re judging me exactly, but I feel like they notice it. I can’t stop seeing it myself—that rough, kind of sunken patch—especially in photos or when I catch it in the mirror. It makes that side of my face look old and beat-up to me.\n\nPeople keep saying they barely see anything, and maybe they mean that, but then I’ll be talking to somebody and I’m thinking, are they looking at it? Like on a job site, guys notice everything—crooked cuts, bad concrete, whatever—so I guess my mind goes there with my face too.\n\nDoctor: When you're worried about people noticing this patch on your face, is it more that you're concerned about what they might think of you as a person, or is it just that you wish the patch wasn't there?\n\nPatient: I guess… it’s more that I just wish it wasn’t there. I mean, I worry what people think too, sure, but it starts with me seeing it. Like in a mirror, or in a photo, that rough, sunken-looking patch just jumps out at me, and then I’m thinking one side of my face looks old and beat-up.\n\nAnd people tell me they barely notice anything, which should help, I know. But it’s like when you’re looking at bad drywall or a crooked frame—you can’t unsee it once you see it. That’s how it feels with my face. It’s just there, and I can’t stop looking at it.\n\nDoctor: Is your main goal in wanting this patch to be different to just have it look better to yourself, or is it also to avoid any potential social or professional consequences?\n\nPatient: I think mostly I just want it to look better to me, if that makes sense. Like, I’m here because I can’t stop seeing it — that rough, kind of sunken patch on my face. In photos or in the mirror it’s just right there, and it makes one side of me look old and beat-up.\n\nI mean, sure, I don’t want people noticing it at work or anything, but even if nobody said a word… I’d still see it. My wife says it’s barely anything, and people tell me they don’t really notice, but that almost makes me feel crazier because I’m standing there staring at it like it’s the first thing on my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if this patch on your face were to disappear?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, I’d like to think so. If that rough, sunken-looking patch was gone, I feel like I wouldn’t keep getting stuck on my face every time I look in the mirror or see a photo.\n\nBut then, you know, I do construction, and sometimes you fix one part of a wall and then another spot starts looking off, so… I don’t know. But this patch is the thing I can’t stop seeing right now. It makes one side of my face look old and beat-up to me, even though everyone keeps saying they barely notice anything. So yeah, I think I’d be a lot more satisfied if it just wasn’t there.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself repeatedly checking or touching other parts of your body besides your face?\n\nPatient: Not really other parts, no. It’s mostly my face—this one spot.\n\nLike before work, or if we’re supposed to go to my sister’s game night, I’ll end up in the bathroom mirror too long, turning my face this way and that under the light. Bathrooms are the worst for it, you know, that overhead lighting makes everything look rough. I’ll rub at the spot too, like I’m trying to smooth it out, which I know doesn’t really do anything.\n\nSometimes I’ll wear my hat lower if I feel like it looks bad. Or I’ll start thinking of some excuse not to go, like I’m tired from work or my back’s acting up, when really it’s because I think that patch is too obvious. But other body parts, not so much. It’s really that face thing.\n\nDoctor: Has your concern about your face affected your ability to work or interact with others in a way that you feel is impacting your overall well-being?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess it has. I don’t like admitting that, because it sounds kind of ridiculous when I say it out loud.\n\nI’ve been late getting out the door for jobs because I’m in the mirror messing with it, checking it under different light, and then suddenly I’m rushing to the construction site. And at work, if they’re taking site photos, I kind of hang back or turn away. I don’t want my face showing up looking like that.\n\nAnd with family stuff too… my sister does these game nights, and sometimes I’ll just skip them or make up some reason, like I’m wiped out from work. Which, I mean, I am tired a lot—construction will do that to you, especially at my age—but really it’s more that I don’t want to sit there feeling like everybody can see that patch on my face. So yeah, it’s been affecting things.\n\nDoctor: Is there something specific about the patch on your face that bothers you, or is it more that you just don't like how your face looks in general?\n\nPatient: It’s more specific, I think. It’s that patch. I mean, I don’t love getting older—who does, right? Working construction, being out in the sun, you start noticing every line and dent eventually. My hands look like sandpaper half the time, but that doesn’t bother me the same way.\n\nWith my face, it’s this rough, kind of sunken-looking spot. In the mirror or in photos, my eye goes right to it, like one side of my face looks old and beat-up. Other people keep telling me they barely notice it, but I don’t know… to me it’s all I can see.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that patch on your face looks the way you think it does to other people?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I mean, people say they barely notice it, and I hear that, but then I catch myself in a photo or in the bathroom mirror and it’s just right there to me. That rough, kind of sunken patch.\n\nIt’s like one side of my face looks older, beat-up, like I’ve been out in the sun and wind too many years. Which, I mean, I have—construction will do that, you’re outside all day, hard hat rubbing, dust everywhere. But this one spot just looks bad to me. Other people keep saying it’s nothing, or they don’t see what I’m talking about, but I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that patch on your face is actually there, or is it something you just see when you look in the mirror?\n\nPatient: I think it’s there… I mean, I don’t think I’m making it up out of nothing. When I look in the mirror, especially in certain light, or if I see a photo of myself, I can see that rough, kind of sunken-looking patch. It’s like one side of my face just looks old and beat-up.\n\nBut then other people say they barely notice anything, so I don’t know if it’s as bad as I’m seeing it. You know, I’ve worked outside for years, sun and dust and all that, so part of me thinks, well, of course my face is going to show it. But this one spot… I can’t stop seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think that patch on your face would still be there if you couldn't see yourself in mirrors or photos?\n\nPatient: I guess… yeah, I think it would still be there. I mean, I’m here because I can’t stop seeing it when I do see myself — in the mirror, in photos, that rough, kind of sunken patch. It’s like one side of my face looks old and beat-up.\n\nIf I couldn’t look, maybe I wouldn’t be staring at it all the time, you know? Like when I’m on a job and I’m busy hauling stuff or cutting boards, I’m not thinking about my face every second. But then I catch my reflection in a truck window or someone shows me a picture, and there it is again. Other people say they barely notice anything, but to me it’s just… right there.\n\nDoctor: Do you think the patch on your face would look different to you if nobody else could see it?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… maybe if nobody else could see it, I’d still see it. That’s kind of the problem, I guess. I’m here because I can’t stop noticing that rough, sunken-looking patch when I look in the mirror or see myself in photos.\n\nPeople keep telling me it’s barely anything, and maybe they’re right, but to me it makes that side of my face look old, beat-up. Like after years on job sites, sun hitting you all day, dust sticking to your skin—sorry, I know that’s not exactly what you asked. I just mean… even if no one else saw it, I think I’d still be stuck seeing it.\n\nDoctor: Do you think a dermatologist or plastic surgeon could fix the patch on your face to your satisfaction?\n\nPatient: I don’t know. I mean, I’ve thought about it, sure, like maybe there’s some cream or laser thing or… I don’t know what they even do for that. My cousin had something done on his nose once, not the same thing, he got hit playing softball years ago and it always bothered him—\n\nBut for me, I’m not sure if I’d be satisfied. Maybe if someone could smooth it out or make it look less sunken, that would help. But then I worry I’d still look at it and think it’s not right. I guess I’d have to talk to someone who actually knows skin, because I’m just looking at it every day and driving myself nuts with it.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

Yeah… I think it probably would still look bad to me. I mean, if a doctor said there’s nothing wrong, I’d hear that, sure. I’m not saying I know more than a doctor.

I mean, I worry what people think too, sure, but it starts with me seeing it. Like, I’m here because I can’t stop seeing it — that rough, kind of sunken patch on my face. In photos or in the mirror it’s just right there, and it makes one side of me look old and beat-up.\n\nI mean, sure, I don’t want people noticing it at work or anything, but even if nobody said a word… I’d still see it. My wife says it’s barely anything, and people tell me they don’t really notice, but that almost makes me feel crazier because I’m standing there staring at it like it’s the first thing on my face.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be satisfied with your appearance if this patch on your face were to disappear?\n\nPatient: Maybe… I mean, I’d like to think so. If that rough, sunken-looking patch was gone, I feel like I wouldn’t keep getting stuck on my face every time I look in the mirror or see a photo.\n\nBut then, you know, I do construction, and sometimes you fix one part of a wall and then another spot starts looking off, so… I don’t know.
